# Korean 02 coverage matrix

## Stage purpose

- Locale and variety: `ko-KR`, contemporary standard South Korean.
- Default productive register: ordinary polite `해요체`. Productive formal polite `합니다체` is limited to the public, school, workplace, presentation, telephone, reception, announcement, and service settings assigned below.
- Canonical learner input: standard Hangul spelling and spacing only. Korean learner-facing text contains no romanization, Hanja study, or phonetic respelling.
- Planned size: exactly 1,280 original cards, `ko-02-0001` through `ko-02-1280`.
- Structure: thirty-two binding fresh-context slices of exactly 40 cards. The content is cumulative under the prerequisite graph; the ID range alone never establishes completion.
- Entry point: all 300 Korean 00 cards and all 1,000 Korean 01 cards. Those 1,300 accepted cards are an immutable prerequisite and duplicate-comparison inventory, never templates, paraphrase sources, continuations, or style seeds.
- Outcome: a complete strong-beginner operating system for ability, obligation, conditions, intentions, attempts, experience, temporal sequence, comparison, limits, noun modification, context-bound formal polite speech, introductory quotation, controlled expanded honorification, short narration, practical messages, and standard-spelling recovery from careful and ordinary connected delivery.

Every card has one focused, normalized-unique production target in a complete fair situation. The quotas below reserve substantive practice for each required contrast and task; they do not authorize paradigms, headword lists, or mechanical substitutions.

## 1. Grammar and constructions

- Ability and inability with `-(으)ㄹ 수 있어요/없어요`, plus inherited `못` for inability, failed performance, or obstructing circumstances.
- Practical obligation and necessity with `-아/어야 해요` and `-아/어야 돼요`; controlled ordinary rules and prohibitions use already-established language and, after the conditional is available, `-(으)면 안 돼요`.
- Real and likely conditions with `-(으)면` for plans, choices, procedures, availability, instructions, and ordinary consequences.
- Intention with `-(으)려고 해요` and motivated past `-(으)려고 했어요`, contrasted with inherited expected or planned future `-(으)ㄹ 거예요`.
- Attempts with `-아/어 봐요`, `-아/어 보세요`, and motivated `-아/어 봤어요`; prior experience with `-아/어 본 적이 있어요/없어요`.
- Temporal ordering with `-기 전에`, `-(으)ㄴ 후에`, `-고 나서`, and established clock or calendar expressions followed by `전` or `후`.
- Comparison with `보다`, `더`, `덜`, and `제일/가장`; controlled similarity or equality with `처럼` and beginner-useful `만큼`.
- Limits with `밖에` plus a required negative predicate, contrasted with inherited `만`, `부터`, and `까지`.
- Productive noun-modifying clauses: present action `-는`, past action `-(으)ㄴ`, descriptive `-(으)ㄴ`, future/intended/expected `-(으)ㄹ`, and high-frequency `있는/없는` and irregular forms.
- Productive formal polite declaratives `-습니다/ㅂ니다`, motivated formal past, formal copular `입니다/아닙니다`, questions `-습니까/ㅂ니까` and `입니까`, and a small public/service set with `-(으)십시오` or `-아/어 주십시오`.
- Introductory quotation: action statement forms in the `-다고 해요` family, noun `-(이)라고 해요`, descriptive `-다고 해요`, question `-냐고 해요`, instruction/request `-(으)라고 해요`, and suggestion `-자고 해요`.
- Reinforced subject honorification `-시-`; controlled `계시다`, `드시다`, `주무시다`, `말씀하시다`; inherited respectful personal-information forms; and only the fixed humble chunks listed in the register inventory.
- Connected discourse remains two to four short events or one to three short message sentences. It reuses established `-고`, `-아서/어서`, `-지만`, `그리고`, `그래서`, and `그런데` alongside the assigned Korean 02 constructions.

## 2. Communicative functions

- State and ask about practical skill, access, availability, and inability; distinguish lacking ability from an action that failed on one occasion.
- State personal necessities, situational requirements, deadlines, procedures, household duties, health routines, and ordinary rules without issuing blunt obligations to another adult.
- Give a real condition for a plan, choice, instruction, service option, schedule, route, weather response, or ordinary consequence.
- State a near-term intention, ask about another adult's plan appropriately, and explain a changed or abandoned intention.
- Suggest trying an action, report one actual attempt, and exchange prior experience in food, travel, study, work, activities, services, and technology.
- Put preparation and follow-up around an event; describe two to four completed events in a clear short order.
- Compare prices, products, sizes, quality, routes, travel times, schedules, quantities, places, and options; state restricted stock, time, money, quantity, or availability.
- Identify useful people, objects, places, events, information, and plans with present, past, descriptive, or future noun-modifying clauses.
- Present, receive, ask, announce, report, and serve in justified formal-polite settings, while returning to ordinary polite speech in peer conversation.
- Report one short statement, identity, question, instruction, request, or suggestion with an explicit source and recoverable recipient.
- Speak respectfully about teachers, older relatives, managers, customers, and guests; keep the honored grammatical subject distinct from the listener and speaker.
- Write and understand appointment changes, invitations, phone or text messages, notices, announcements, school/work updates, procedure reminders, and clarification after a reported message.

## 3. Core lexical domains

- Skills, operation, access, reservations, connectivity, document or device use, and practical availability.
- Duties, preparation, deadlines, appointments, requirements, health routines, travel rules, household responsibilities, and school/work procedures.
- Plans, intentions, choices, changes of plan, weather-dependent actions, and schedule-dependent options.
- Attempts and experience with food, travel, cultural activities, sports, services, study, work tasks, applications, and common technology.
- Preparation, departure, arrival, completion, follow-up, before/after time, and short event sequences.
- Products, models, sizes, prices, quality, stock, routes, distance, travel time, schedules, quantity, money, and service options.
- People, objects, places, events, plans, schedules, messages, notices, and information serving as noun-phrase heads.
- Reception, classroom, school office, workplace, presentation, formal telephone, customer service, public notice, and transport announcement settings.
- Family, age, teachers, managers, customers, guests, meals, sleep, presence, words, and respectful personal information.
- Invitations, instructions, suggestions, appointment changes, delivery or repair status, absence, delay, completion, and practical outcomes.
- Pronunciation-sensitive high-frequency vocabulary inherited from Korean 00–01 plus new useful collocations that naturally support Korean 02; no separate phonology word list.

An item is only planned here. It counts as lexical breadth after it occurs naturally in an independently accepted card and enters the lexical/chunk index.

## 4. Collocations and chunks

Required reusable families include:

- using a device or service, making a reservation, entering a place, finding time, and completing a practical task;
- meeting a deadline, preparing documents, following a procedure, keeping an appointment, taking medicine, and doing a household responsibility;
- acting if weather, stock, time, price, health, transport, or service availability meets a real condition;
- intending to call, visit, apply, reserve, change, buy, prepare, or attend, including one credible changed intention;
- trying a food, feature, route, study method, service, or task, and asking whether someone has had a relevant prior experience;
- checking something before departure or submission, completing a follow-up after an event, and finishing one action before the next;
- comparing a price, size, duration, route, schedule, product, or service and choosing on a stated practical basis;
- having only a limited quantity, time, budget, stock choice, seat, appointment, or service window;
- finding the person who handles a task, the item bought earlier, a suitable place, an available day, or a plan to follow;
- opening a short presentation, handling reception, asking a formal question, giving a brief status report, and delivering a limited public instruction;
- respectfully asking or reporting where someone is, whether the person has eaten or slept, what the person said, or when the person will arrive;
- reporting what one source said, asked, requested, instructed, or suggested, then confirming or correcting the message;
- updating a peer or group about a delay, appointment change, work/school task, household problem, service outcome, or travel sequence.

These are semantic families, not preapproved answer strings. A generator must vary communicative purpose and argument structure, not fill a quota by changing one verb, noun, name, date, number, or ending.

## 5. Pronunciation and orthography

- Reuse the complete Korean 00 sound-and-spelling foundation and Korean 01 standard-spacing control.
- Recur through plain, aspirated, and tense consonants; major vowel spellings; simple and common complex 받침; resyllabification; nasalization; tensification; aspiration involving `ㅎ`; and `ㄴ/ㄹ` lateralization inside meaningful stage tasks.
- Hear ordinary contractions in accepted polite speech while typing standard orthographic forms. A contracted form counts only when it is the natural standard form, not a phonetic imitation.
- Preserve construction boundaries and spacing in `못`, `-(으)ㄹ 수 있어요`, `-아/어야 해요`, `-아/어야 돼요`, `-(으)려고 해요`, `-아/어 본 적이 있어요`, `-기 전에`, and `-(으)ㄴ 후에`.
- Preserve noun-modifier attachment to the predicate and the space before its head noun; make the modifier boundary audible without inserting written material.
- Group number/counter and comparison phrases naturally while retaining standard number words, counter spacing, particles, and underlying consonants.
- Make quotation source, quoted-content, and reporting-predicate boundaries audible; do not store a change of voice, pause, or phonetic form as an answer.
- Distinguish statement, question, instruction, suggestion, and report intonation through wording, situation, punctuation, and contour together.
- Practice formal-ending delivery and subject-honorific boundaries without spelling pronunciation.
- Contrast careful textbook-like delivery with ordinary connected delivery; both map to one standard written answer.
- Retain `?` when a question or invitation needs an unambiguous synthesis cue. Terminal punctuation stays outside `cloze` and `answer`.

The binding recurring assignments appear in the pronunciation inventory below.

## 6. Register

- Ordinary polite `해요체` is the default for unfamiliar adults, adult peers, classmates, colleagues, family interactions that remain polite, and routine service exchanges.
- Productive `합니다체` is used only for the contexts enumerated in the binding register inventory. Formality follows the setting and audience, not a word-for-word conversion exercise.
- A formal utterance keeps one speech level throughout. A card may contrast two separate contexts or turns only when the boundary and speakers are explicit; one utterance never mixes formal and ordinary endings accidentally.
- `저` is used when an explicit polite first-person reference matters and omitted when recoverable. Names, titles, roles, and kinship terms replace unnatural repeated pronouns.
- `이름+씨` remains for similar-status adults. The controlled productive title set is `선생님`, `교수님`, `팀장님`, `사장님`, `고객님`, `손님`, `부모님`, `할머니`, and `할아버지`, each only in its assigned natural relationship.
- Subject `-시-` honors the grammatical subject, not merely the listener. Never honor the speaker's own action.
- Fixed humble language is restricted to `말씀드려요`, `말씀드립니다`, and `확인해 드립니다` in the exact contexts below. These chunks do not open a productive humble-verb or benefactive system.
- No casual intimate speech, bureaucratic written style, broad hierarchy language, slang, dialect, aggressive wording, or character-like speech is productive.

## 7. Pragmatics

- `못` can frame inability, failed performance, or blocking circumstances; `-(으)ㄹ 수 없어요` presents lack of possibility or capacity. Context, not an English gloss, decides the natural framing.
- `-아/어야 해요` and `-아/어야 돼요` are often ordinary near-equivalents. The matrix teaches accepted use and register tendency, not a false categorical semantic split.
- Address obligations to another adult as an impersonal rule, a question about what the speaker must do, or an appropriately softened reminder. Avoid bossy decontextualized mandates.
- A condition gives a possible trigger; `-아서/어서` gives an actual reason or tightly linked sequence. The result and time reference must make the choice recoverable.
- `-(으)려고 해요` foregrounds the actor's intention; `-(으)ㄹ 거예요` gives an expected or planned future. Do not claim a stronger distinction than the scene supports.
- An attempt is one act of trying; experience concerns whether such an event has occurred before. A noun swap does not establish the contrast.
- `-고 나서` requires completion before the following event; `-(으)ㄴ 후에` marks a later time more neutrally; `-기 전에` locates an earlier action.
- `밖에` requires a negative predicate and presents a restricted remainder. `만` directly limits; `부터/까지` mark boundaries rather than small quantity.
- Comparison avoids insults, body judgments, or implausible ranking of people. `처럼` marks similarity, while `만큼` marks a clear degree or equality relation in its limited beginner use.
- Every form-sensitive person card makes speaker, listener, grammatical subject, and relationship recoverable. A reported speech act also identifies the source and intended recipient.
- Instructions, suggestions, reported requests, and reported statements must remain distinct in actor, force, and form.
- Omit recoverable participants naturally. Keep reported messages short enough that there is no ambiguity about who said what to whom.

## 8. Listening targets

- Distinguish ability, circumstantial failure, and lack of possibility from the full event context.
- Hear obligation, conditional, intention, attempt, experience, temporal-order, comparison, and limit boundaries in normal careful beginner speech and ordinary connected delivery.
- Distinguish a real condition from a stated reason; an intention from an expected plan; one attempt from prior experience; and the three before/after relations.
- Recover the predicate class and time/aspect of a noun modifier, identify its head noun, and reject an interpretation that attaches the clause to the wrong noun.
- Distinguish ordinary polite and justified formal polite speech, including declarative, question, limited instruction, announcement, and formal telephone contours.
- Identify the respected grammatical subject independently of the listener and recognize the controlled honorific lexemes in connected delivery.
- Segment one short reported statement, identity, question, instruction, request, or suggestion into source, content, and reporting predicate.
- Follow two to four connected events or one to three message sentences while keeping one target span recoverable.
- Recover standard number words, counters, comparison grouping, spelling, and punctuation despite sound changes and ordinary contraction.

## 9. Production targets

- Type one focused target in standard Hangul spelling and spacing with no transliteration or phonetic alternative.
- Select an ability, obligation, condition, intention, attempt, experience, temporal-order, comparison, or limit construction from a concrete practical situation.
- Build a useful complete noun phrase with the correct predicate class, time/aspect, modifier ending, and head noun.
- Produce formal declaratives, questions, and the limited public/service instructions only where the setting warrants them; otherwise maintain ordinary polite speech.
- Report one short unambiguous speech act with the correct introductory quotation form and explicit source/recipient relationship.
- Choose ordinary or subject-honorific wording for a recoverable subject; use only the controlled lexical honorific and fixed humble inventory.
- Produce a short practical message, update, notice, announcement, or two-to-four-event account without hiding a whole passage as the target.
- Preserve standard underlying spelling, number/counter spacing, quotation and noun-modifier boundaries, and required punctuation through careful and connected delivery.
- Complete cumulative strong-beginner listening-to-typing tasks without importing Korean 03 discourse, stance, voice, or inference systems.

## 10. Prerequisites

- Stage entry requires Korean 00 slices 1–10 and Korean 01 slices 1–25 in full. This includes direct Hangul input, the accepted sound system, ordinary polite present/past/future, particles, numbers/counters, daily domains, `안/못`, requests, short connectors, basic subject honorification, and all 1,300 protected primary targets.
- Korean 00–01 words and constructions may support a new situation, but their sentences are never copied, lightly expanded, continued, or paraphrased. Their answers remain protected against exact, normalized, semantic, and disguised mechanical duplication.
- The Korean 02 graph is a cumulative linear chain. Slice 1 uses Korean 00–01 only; every later slice may use Korean 00–01 plus accepted material from all numerically earlier Korean 02 slices and no other Korean.

```text
Slice 1 → Slice 2 → Slice 3 → Slice 4 → Slice 5 → Slice 6 → Slice 7 → Slice 8
        → Slice 9 → Slice 10 → Slice 11 → Slice 12 → Slice 13 → Slice 14 → Slice 15 → Slice 16
        → Slice 17 → Slice 18 → Slice 19 → Slice 20 → Slice 21 → Slice 22 → Slice 23 → Slice 24
        → Slice 25 → Slice 26 → Slice 27 → Slice 28 → Slice 29 → Slice 30 → Slice 31 → Slice 32
```

- Planned material is not usable support. It becomes a prerequisite only after its card passes fresh independent Korean-editor review, source-contract validation, and the cumulative duplicate audit.

### Binding prerequisite inventory

| Prerequisite source | Material available as support | Binding limitation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Korean 00 slices 1–10 | direct Hangul input, standard spelling recovery, consonant/vowel/받침 control, connected-sound families, phrase grouping, punctuation contours, and accepted fixed survival expressions | Fixed expressions remain immutable support; they do not license productive grammar or new target reuse. |
| Korean 01 slices 1–16 | copula/existence, particles, present action/descriptive predicates, deictics, complete practical number/counter foundation, identity/family/home/routine/food/shopping/transport/school/work/weather/health domains | No Korean 01 sentence, answer, or mechanical expansion becomes a Korean 02 target. |
| Korean 01 slices 17–23 | past, expected future, desire/invitations, `안/못`, requests/instructions, `-고`, `-아서/어서`, `-지만`, and short sentence connectors | These forms support Korean 02 contrasts but do not count as new Korean 02 coverage by themselves. |
| Korean 01 slices 24–25 | basic subject `-시-`, controlled respectful lexemes/titles, integrated TOPIK 1 operation, sound/spacing/relationship audits | Expanded use must stay within the Korean 02 register inventory; inherited fixed formal chunks are not productive formal paradigms. |
| Accepted earlier Korean 02 slices | only final cards and index rows from every numerically lower slice explicitly named in the current slice | Planned, draft, rejected, same-slice, and later-slice language is unavailable as support. |
| Protected comparison corpus | all 1,300 Korean 00–01 normalized answers plus all accepted earlier Korean 02 targets and semantic signatures | It is used for duplicate rejection only, never as a template, continuation, paraphrase bank, or style seed. |

## 11. Exclusions

- No productive `-(으)ㄹ 줄 알다/모르다` or second ability system; no advanced hypothetical, concessive, or counterfactual condition system.
- No purpose or commitment system beyond listed `-(으)려고 해요`; no expanded attempt or experience paradigm.
- No advanced simultaneity or before/after system and no long itinerary or multi-event narration.
- No productive noun nominalization beyond forms internally required by the listed constructions; no productive `-는데` discourse management.
- No advanced cause/consequence, inference, evidentiality, hearsay stance, productive `-(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다`, `-나 보다`, advanced `-겠-` stance, or stance-bearing sentence endings.
- No passive or causative system; no full indirect-speech tense system, future-quotation system, nested quotation, source ambiguity, or long reported discourse.
- No broad honorific/humble conversion, business hierarchy, indirect refusal, advanced request softening, or bureaucratic/institutional written style.
- No abstract discussion, argument, specialized legal or medical language, slang, aggressive language, dialect production, or strongly gendered/character-like speech.
- No Hanja study, romanization, Korean `reading` field, phonetic answer, alternate phonetic accepted answer, pronunciation score, ASR, or forced alignment.
- No mechanical form, tense, polarity, head-noun, name, country, food, activity, number, date, price, size, route, or counter substitution as independent coverage.
- No Korean 00–01 target reuse, arbitrary larger cloze hiding a duplicate, long complete-sentence cloze, or cloze crossing punctuation, quotation, or a speaker-turn boundary.
- No Korean 03 vocabulary or construction introduced as support merely to make a Korean 02 target sound more elaborate.

### Binding exclusion inventory

| Reserved or prohibited area | Rejection boundary |
| --- | --- |
| Alternative ability, advanced condition, purpose, and commitment systems | Reject productive `-(으)ㄹ 줄 알다/모르다`, counterfactual/concessive/hypothetical expansion, and any purpose/promise system beyond listed intention forms. |
| Nominalization and discourse management | Reject productive nominalization beyond internal pieces of required constructions, productive `-는데`, advanced simultaneity, and long connector chains. |
| Inference, evidentiality, and stance | Reject inference/hearsay systems including productive `-(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다` and `-나 보다`, advanced `-겠-` stance, stance-bearing endings, and reporting used to imply unassigned evidential distance. |
| Voice and valency | Reject passive/causative systems and sentences that require them to understand the primary target. |
| Expanded quotation | Reject future-quotation paradigms, full tense shifting, nested sources, long direct/indirect reports, ambiguous roles, and advanced hearsay stance. |
| Expanded honorifics and workplace language | Reject broad honorific/humble conversion, new humble verbs/chunks, hierarchy systems, indirect refusal, advanced request softening, and bureaucratic institutional prose. |
| Long or abstract discourse | Reject more than four narrated events, argument, abstract discussion, long passage clozes, specialized regulation, and advanced medical/legal language. |
| Nonstandard variety or representation | Reject slang, aggression, dialect production, character-like/gendered speech, Hanja study, romanization, a Korean `reading` field, and phonetic respelling. |
| Pronunciation infrastructure | Reject alternate phonetic answers, pronunciation scoring, ASR, forced alignment, extra voices, or a new phonology framework. |
| Duplication and padding | Reject target reuse, larger-span disguise, and form/name/noun/activity/country/number/date/price/route/size/counter/modifier-head/quotation-source/speech-level substitutions without independent function. |
| Workflow/infrastructure artifacts | Reject prompts, transcripts, review state, reports, content databases, generalized runners/frameworks, generated binaries, committed audio, and archives. |

## Binding grammar and contrast inventory

### Construction assignments

| Construction | Productive scope | Required contrast or boundary | Slice(s) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `-(으)ㄹ 수 있어요` | Practical learned or situational ability and access; statements, questions, and short answers. | Inherited `못`; no `-(으)ㄹ 줄 알다/모르다`. | 1–2, 31–32 |
| `-(으)ㄹ 수 없어요` | Lack of capacity, permission-like possibility only when framed as availability, or objective impossibility. | `못` failure/circumstance; inherited fixed prohibition does not equal inability. | 1–2, 31–32 |
| `못` | Inherited short form for inability, failed performance, and blocking circumstances. | `-(으)ㄹ 수 없어요`; inherited `안` remains volitional/factual negation. | 2, 6, 31–32 |
| `-아/어야 해요` | Personal necessity, practical requirement, deadline, health routine, and neutral rule statement. | `-아/어야 돼요`; soften adult-directed obligations. | 3–4, 8, 29–32 |
| `-아/어야 돼요` | Common spoken obligation/requirement in the same accepted range. | No false categorical meaning split from `-아/어야 해요`; formal rules wait for assigned formal language. | 3–4, 29–32 |
| `-(으)면 안 돼요` | Controlled ordinary prohibition after the real conditional is established. | Condition plus inherited `안 돼요`; no legalistic prohibition system. | 6, 29, 31–32 |
| `-(으)면` | Real/likely condition for plans, choices, procedures, availability, instructions, and consequences. | Actual reason/linked sequence `-아서/어서`; no counterfactuals. | 5–6, 8, 16, 29–32 |
| `-(으)려고 해요` | Present intention of a recoverable actor. | Expected/planned future `-(으)ㄹ 거예요`; no purpose-clause expansion. | 7–8, 29–32 |
| `-(으)려고 했어요` | A prior intention that changed, was interrupted, or did not proceed, when the outcome is stated. | Past plan is not itself an attempt; no long explanation. | 8, 29–32 |
| `-아/어 봐요` | Ordinary suggestion to try an action. | Actual attempt and accumulated experience. | 9, 29, 31–32 |
| `-아/어 보세요` | Polite try-suggestion to an appropriate adult or service user. | Not a blunt command and not subject honorification. | 9, 25, 29, 31–32 |
| `-아/어 봤어요` | One motivated completed attempt. | `-아/어 본 적이 있어요`; no noun-substitution series. | 9–10, 30–32 |
| `-아/어 본 적이 있어요/없어요` | Prior experience or lack of experience in practical domains. | One attempt `-아/어 봤어요`; no frequency or expertise inference. | 10, 30–32 |
| `-기 전에` | Action performed before another event/action. | Later-time forms; internal `-기` does not open nominalization. | 11, 13, 29–32 |
| `-(으)ㄴ 후에` | Neutral time after a completed action or event. | Stronger completed sequence `-고 나서`; not a conjugation table. | 12–13, 29–32 |
| `-고 나서` | Completion of the first action before the following action, normally with a clear event chain. | Neutral `-(으)ㄴ 후에`; no long narrative. | 13, 29–32 |
| Clock/calendar `전/후` | Useful clock, date, deadline, arrival, departure, and appointment offsets. | Clause-level before/after forms; inherited `부터/까지`. | 11–13, 31–32 |
| Comparative `보다` | Compare two recoverable options or a standard across practical attributes. | Similarity `처럼`; equality/degree `만큼`; particle attaches to comparison standard. | 14–15, 31–32 |
| `더` and `덜` | Greater or lesser degree/quantity when the scale is clear. | No adjective substitution drill; avoid harmful personal judgments. | 14, 16, 31–32 |
| `제일/가장` | Practical superlative among an explicit or recoverable set. | No false universal claim or ranking padding. | 15, 31–32 |
| `처럼` | Concrete similarity in manner or appearance. | Comparative `보다` and degree/equality `만큼`; no extended metaphor. | 15, 31–32 |
| `만큼` | Clear beginner equality or degree with a stated standard. | No `-(으)ㄹ 만큼`, proportional correlatives, or abstract degree discourse. | 15, 31–32 |
| `밖에` plus negative | Restricted remaining time, stock, quantity, money, people, or options. | Positive/restrictive `만`; boundaries `부터/까지`. | 16, 29, 31–32 |
| Present action modifier `-는` | Current, habitual, or generally identifying action modifying a useful head noun. | Past action and future/intended modifiers. | 17, 21, 29–32 |
| Past action modifier `-(으)ㄴ` | Completed action identifying a useful head noun. | Descriptive present with the same surface family; time reference must be explicit. | 18, 21, 29–32 |
| Descriptive modifier `-(으)ㄴ` | Current quality/state modifying a useful head noun. | Past action modifier; predicate class controls meaning. | 19, 21, 29–32 |
| Future/intended modifier `-(으)ㄹ` | Intended, expected, scheduled, or not-yet-completed action modifying a useful head noun. | Sentence-final future; no advanced conjectural stance. | 20–21, 29–32 |
| `있는/없는` and irregular modifier forms | High-frequency existence/availability and accepted irregular predicates inside complete noun phrases. | No mechanical paradigm or rare irregular padding. | 17–21, 29–32 |
| Formal declarative `-습니다/ㅂ니다` | Present and motivated past action/descriptive statements in assigned public/formal contexts. | Default `해요체`; no bureaucratic written style. | 22–23, 25, 29, 31–32 |
| Formal copula `입니다/아닙니다` | Identification, opening, correction, and brief formal status. | Ordinary `이에요/예요/아니에요`; no sentence-by-sentence conversion. | 22–23, 31–32 |
| Formal question `-습니까/ㅂ니까`, `입니까` | Reception, formal telephone, school/work, presentation, and service questions. | Ordinary polite questions; subject honorification added only when justified. | 23, 25, 31–32 |
| Limited `-(으)십시오`, `-아/어 주십시오` | Public, safety, announcement, and reception instructions from an authorized role. | No broad imperative or superior-directed use. | 23, 29, 31–32 |
| Action statement quotation family | Short nonpast `-ㄴ/는다고 해요` and a small motivated completed `-았/었다고 해요` set under the required `-다고 해요` family. | Ordinary unreported statement; no future or full tense-shift system. | 26, 29–32 |
| Noun quotation `-(이)라고 해요` | Report a short identity, label, name, place, date, or role. | Formal copula and ordinary direct identification. | 26, 29–32 |
| Descriptive quotation `-다고 해요` | Report one short quality/state claim. | Action form selection; no hearsay stance or evaluation chain. | 26, 29–32 |
| Question quotation `-냐고 해요` | Report one short information, yes/no, descriptive, or noun question with accepted surface selection. | Ordinary question; source and recipient explicit. | 27, 29–32 |
| Instruction/request quotation `-(으)라고 해요` | Report one short instruction or request and its intended actor. | Ordinary instruction/request; suggestion `-자고 해요`. | 28–29, 31–32 |
| Suggestion quotation `-자고 해요` | Report one short joint-action suggestion. | Instruction, request, or actor intention. | 28–29, 31–32 |
| Subject `-시-` | Reinforced present, past, modifier, planned, formal, and quotation contexts with a recoverable respected subject. | Ordinary subject wording; never self-honorification or blanket listener marking. | 20–21, 24–29, 31–32 |
| `계시다`, `드시다`, `주무시다`, `말씀하시다` | Controlled high-frequency presence, eating, sleeping, and speaking contexts. | Ordinary counterparts; no broad lexical conversion or double honorific. | 24–25, 29, 31–32 |
| Fixed humble chunks | Only `말씀드려요`, `말씀드립니다`, and `확인해 드립니다` in the enumerated source/recipient contexts. | No productive humble/benefactive system. | 25, 29, 31–32 |

### Binding contrast map

| Required contrast | Recoverable situational distinction | Introduction and cumulative audit |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `못` versus `-(으)ㄹ 수 없어요` | A failed/blocked performance or ordinary inability versus a capacity/possibility framing; use paired situations only when both are natural and the framing changes. | 1–2; 6, 31–32 |
| `-(으)ㄹ 거예요` versus `-(으)려고 해요` | An expected or planned future versus the actor's current intention; person and evidence remain explicit. | 7–8; 29, 31–32 |
| `-아/어야 해요` versus `-아/어야 돼요` | Both are accepted ordinary obligation forms; context shows personal, external, or situational source without inventing an absolute semantic rule. | 3–4; 29, 31–32 |
| `-(으)면` versus `-아서/어서` | A possible trigger and result versus an actual reason or tightly linked realized sequence. | 5–6; 29–32 |
| `-아/어 보다` versus `-아/어 본 적이 있다` | One suggestion/attempt versus whether a prior experience exists. | 9–10; 30–32 |
| `-기 전에`, `-(으)ㄴ 후에`, `-고 나서` | Earlier action, neutral later time, and completion-dependent following action. | 11–13; 29–32 |
| `만` versus `밖에` | Direct positive restriction versus restricted remainder with a required negative predicate. | 16; 29, 31–32 |
| `만`, `밖에`, `부터`, `까지` | Restriction/only, negative limited remainder, starting boundary, and ending boundary. | inherited 01 plus 16; 31–32 |
| `보다`, `처럼`, `만큼` | comparison on a scale, concrete similarity, and clear equality/degree. | 14–15; 31–32 |
| Present, past/descriptive, future noun modifiers | Predicate class and event time select the form; the head noun and full situation prevent surface-form guessing. | 17–21; 29–32 |
| `해요체` versus productive `합니다체` | Neutral adult interaction versus public/formal distance created by the setting and audience; no arbitrary conversion pairs. | 22–23; 25, 29, 31–32 |
| Ordinary statement versus reported statement/question/instruction/suggestion | Direct speaker commitment versus one explicit source's speech act; actor and recipient determine quotation form. | 26–29; 31–32 |
| Ordinary versus subject-honorific wording | The grammatical subject's relationship to the speaker, not listener status alone, licenses `-시-` or a lexical honorific. | inherited 01, expanded 24–25; 26–29, 31–32 |
| Instruction versus suggestion versus reported request | Addressee action, joint action, and a source's request to an intended actor remain distinct. | 23, 28–29; 31–32 |

## Binding number and counter reinforcement inventory

Korean 02 adds no number system or counter paradigm. It reuses the complete Korean 01 inventory only where the new construction changes a real task. All learner-produced values remain standard Hangul number words; arbitrary numeric variants are rejected.

| Number/counter function | Required Korean 02 use | Grouping, spacing, and contrast | Slice(s) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Native/Sino selection, counter contractions, and zero forms | select the inherited system for quantities, counters, clock/calendar, price, identifiers, measurements, age, and formal/public information | Retain `한/두/세/네/스무`, functional `공/영`, and inherited place-value composition; do not treat an inflected counter form or zero word as a new lexical item. | 1–32; cumulative audit 31–32 |
| Clock time and calendar dates | deadlines, conditions, intended plans, before/after offsets, appointment changes, formal announcements, and reported messages | Preserve inherited native-hour/Sino-minute and calendar selection; distinguish event time, duration, and offset; no date recital. | 3–8, 11–13, 22–23, 26–32 |
| Duration `시간/분/일/주/달/개월` and short-day forms | ability windows, required preparation, travel/service comparison, restricted time, and connected updates | Counter/unit spacing remains standard; duration is not clock time; every value solves a different function. | 1, 3–6, 11–16, 29–32 |
| Occurrence `번` | limited attempts, contact tries, or procedure repetitions only where occurrence count matters | Keep distinct from route identifier `번` and from experience, which does not require a frequency count. | 9–10, 29–32 |
| Quantity counters `개/명/분/마리/권/장/병/잔/대/벌/켤레/인분/그릇/끼` | ability/capacity, requirements, conditions, comparisons, stock limits, meal/animal tasks, respectful service, and reported quantities | Native contractions, respectful `분`, and counter spaces remain stable; no counter swap series. | 1–6, 14–16, 23–29, 31–32 |
| Money `원` and practical prices | spending ability, requirements, price conditions, comparison, budget limits, and service messages | Group place values naturally; `보다/더/덜/밖에` phrase boundaries must be audible; no price-only variants. | 1–6, 14–16, 22–23, 26, 29–32 |
| Route/identifier `번`, ordinal `번째`, floor `층`, room `호` | conditional directions, ordered procedures, before/after movement, comparison, reception, announcement, and reported location | Context distinguishes occurrence from identifier, ordinal from cardinal, and floor from room; standard unit spacing is retained. | 5–6, 11–15, 22–23, 26–32 |
| Temperature `도`, distance `미터/킬로미터`, and weight `그램/킬로그램` | weather/health conditions, route comparison, service availability, stock/limit messages, and short travel or shopping accounts | Keep Hangul unit spelling and standard spaces; select `영` for a zero value and do not create conversion drills. | 5–6, 14–16, 22–23, 29–32 |
| Age `살/세`, respectful `연세`, people `명/분` | controlled honorific questions/reports and formal service or registration settings | `세` is formal/documentary, not honorific by itself; respected subject and relationship license `연세` and `-시-`. | 24–29, 31–32 |
| Telephone and identifier digits | callback ability, appointment change, formal telephone, reported contact detail, and correction | Use `공` only in inherited functions, group meaningful digit blocks, and avoid arbitrary identifier padding. | 1–2, 8, 23, 27, 29, 31–32 |

## Binding noun-modifier inventory

Every accepted modifier card contains a useful noun phrase inside a complete situation. The focus quotas distinguish predicate class, time/aspect, and head noun; changing only the tense or head noun is not independent coverage.

| Modifier type | Predicate class | Time/aspect meaning | Required head-noun types | Representative useful collocations | Prerequisite slices | Common errors to reject | Assigned slices |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `-는` | action verb | current, habitual, ongoing, or role-identifying action | person/role, object, place, time, task, service | `일하는 사람`, `자주 가는 가게`, `지금 쓰는 자료`, `버스가 오는 시간` | 1–16 | using descriptive predicates as if they were action verbs; wrong head attachment; copying a Korean 01 lexicalized modifier | 17, 21, 29–32 |
| `있는/없는` | existential/availability predicate | current presence, possession, availability, or absence | time/day, stock/product, place/facility, person, option | `시간이 있는 날`, `재고가 없는 상품`, `주차장이 있는 식당`, `예약이 없는 손님` | 1–16 | replacing with descriptive `-(으)ㄴ`; leaving the function of existence ambiguous; mechanical polarity pairs | 17, 21, 29–32 |
| `-(으)ㄴ` | action verb | completed event identifying the head noun | purchased/found/prepared object, visited place, completed task, received message, met person | `어제 산 표`, `전에 가 본 식당` only when experience is also licensed, `준비한 자료`, `받은 메시지` | 1–17 | confusing past action with descriptive present; omitting irregular change; tense-only duplication | 18, 21, 29–32 |
| `-(으)ㄴ` | descriptive predicate | current quality or state | product, size/model, route/place, schedule, food, service option, condition | `가격이 싼 가방`, `조용한 자리`, `가까운 역`, `가능한 시간` | 1–18 | adding action `-는`; treating a past action as a property; subjective or insulting person comparison | 19, 21, 29–32 |
| `-(으)ㄹ` | action verb | intended, expected, scheduled, available for future action, or not yet completed | plan/task, bus/train, place, item, day/time, person/role | `내일 할 일`, `탈 버스`, `만날 사람`, `보낼 자료`, `갈 수 있는 날` only after nested support is controlled | 1–19 | using sentence-final future in modifier position; adding conjectural stance; ambiguous actor; mechanical future conversion | 20–21, 29–32 |
| High-frequency irregular action modifiers | `ㄹ`, `ㄷ`, `ㅂ`, `르`, `ㅅ`, `ㅎ`, `하다/되다` only where the accepted verb requires it | the relevant present, completed, or intended reading above | the same practical head types | `사는 곳`, `걸은 길`, `들을 내용`, `만든 자료`, `도운 사람` when the relationship is natural | relevant introduction through 20 | paradigm recital; rare verb padding; nonstandard stem retention or deletion | 17–21, 31–32 |
| High-frequency irregular descriptive modifiers | accepted `ㅂ`, `르`, `ㅅ`, `ㅎ`, `ㄹ` behavior and contractions | current quality/state | weather, health, product, route, size, condition | `추운 날`, `빠른 길`, `나은 방법`, `어려운 문제`, `파란 표지` | 1–18 | rare-item lists; sound-based misspelling; confusing descriptive present with past action | 19, 21, 31–32 |
| Honorific noun modifiers | action or honorific lexical verb with justified `-시-` | current, completed, or intended action by a respected subject | teacher/customer/older-relative task, place, time, message, plan | `선생님이 말씀하신 내용`, `할머니가 드실 음식`, `팀장님이 계신 곳` | 1–20, 24 | self-honorification; double `-시-`; honoring listener instead of subject; unclear subject | 21, 24–29, 31–32 |

## Binding quotation inventory

Quotation remains introductory: one explicit source, one short reported message, no nested quotation, and no ambiguity about source, recipient, or intended actor. Korean quotation content uses ordinary punctuation in `sentence`; the cloze cannot cross a quotation or sentence boundary.

| Quotation form | Source → recipient relationship | Speech act and content | Punctuation behavior | Assigned slices |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Action `-ㄴ/는다고 해요` | adult peer/colleague/classmate → current adult listener; staff → customer when ordinary polite distance fits | one current, habitual, scheduled-as-present, or generally stated action | Report sentence ends with `.`; a visible direct-source sentence, if used as context, is separate and outside the cloze. | 26, 29–32 |
| Limited past action `-았/었다고 해요` | traveler, coworker, classmate, family member, or service source → current listener | one completed arrival, change, cancellation, submission, purchase, or service result | Report ends with `.` or `?` only when the current speaker asks for confirmation; no tense-shift series. | 26–27, 29–32 |
| Noun `-(이)라고 해요` | named person, receptionist, teacher, coworker, or written notice as explicit source → adult listener | identity, label, role, place, date, room, or short named item | Particle selection follows final sound; terminal punctuation belongs to the whole report, not the target. | 26–27, 29–32 |
| Descriptive `-다고 해요` | customer, colleague, teacher, family member, or service source → adult listener | one short claim about price, difficulty, availability, condition, size, weather, or quality | Report boundary remains audible; no stance or agreement is inferred from reporting. | 26, 29–32 |
| Question `-냐고 해요` family | teacher/manager/staff/peer/relative as source → a clearly identified recipient | one information, yes/no, descriptive, or noun question | The original question force is encoded by the quotation form; the whole report normally ends with `.`, or `?` only if the current utterance is itself a question. | 27, 29–32 |
| Instruction/request `-(으)라고 해요` | teacher → student, manager → worker, staff → customer, family member → speaker, or peer → peer where appropriate | one short instruction or request with an explicit intended actor | Do not retain an original `?` inside the cloze; current report punctuation reflects the current speech act. | 28–29, 31–32 |
| Suggestion `-자고 해요` | adult peer/classmate/colleague/family member → group member | one joint invitation or suggestion about meeting, eating, traveling, checking, changing, or trying | Whole report normally ends with `.`; a confirmation question retains final `?` outside the target. | 28–29, 31–32 |
| Formal-source report | presenter, receptionist, school/work office, or public announcement → adult listener | report one short formal statement or instruction in ordinary narrator `해요체` unless the current reporting setting independently requires `합니다체` | Speech levels belong to distinct source and narrator roles; never mix them inside one utterance. | 26–29, 31–32 |
| Honorific-source report | speaker reports teacher, manager, customer, or older relative as source or grammatical subject | one short statement/question/request with only justified subject honorification | Source title and subject remain recoverable; no double honorific or honorific marking of the current speaker. | 26–29, 31–32 |

## Binding register and honorification inventory

The following list is exhaustive for productive Korean 02 register-sensitive material. Other formal or humble forms may appear only as immutable Korean 00–01 support and cannot become Korean 02 targets.

| Speaker → listener; grammatical subject | Productive form/title/address | Why appropriate | Slice(s) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Adult peer/classmate/colleague → adult peer; ordinary participant is subject | ordinary polite `해요체`, natural omission, optional `이름+씨` | Default neutral polite relationship. | 1–32 |
| Presenter/student/worker → audience or class; speaker, project, event, or factual topic is subject | present `-습니다/ㅂ니다`, motivated past `-았습니다/었습니다`, `입니다/아닙니다` | Public delivery and organized status report warrant formal distance. | 22, 29, 31–32 |
| Receptionist/service staff → customer/visitor; service, booking, office, or neutral fact is subject | `-습니다/ㅂ니다`, `입니다/아닙니다`; `고객님` or `손님` only as natural address/reference | Customer-facing opening, identification, and concise information. | 22–23, 25, 29, 31–32 |
| Adult caller → school/work/service office; caller or call purpose is subject | formal telephone opening with `입니다`; formal factual statement/question | Formal opening identifies caller and purpose without broad telephone script. | 22–23, 29, 31–32 |
| Audience/customer/visitor → presenter/reception/service staff; ordinary topic is subject | `-습니까/ㅂ니까`, `입니까` | A formal question matches the public or reception setting. | 23, 31–32 |
| Receptionist/announcer/authorized staff → public/customer; listener is intended actor | limited `-(으)십시오`, `-아/어 주십시오` | Concise public, safety, queue, document, or boarding instruction from an authorized role. | 23, 29, 31–32 |
| Student → teacher/professor; `선생님` or `교수님` is subject | title plus `-시-`, `계시다`, or `말씀하시다`; ordinary or formal ending follows current setting | Respected educator is the grammatical subject. | 24–29, 31–32 |
| Worker → manager or coworker; `팀장님` or contextually natural `사장님` is subject | title plus `-시-`, `계시다`, `드시다`, or `말씀하시다` as applicable | Respectful workplace reference without building a hierarchy system. | 24–29, 31–32 |
| Service staff → coworker/customer; `고객님` or `손님` is subject | title plus `-시-`; formal question may use `-셨습니까` only when the customer is subject | Controlled respectful service reference; not every service verb is honorified. | 24–29, 31–32 |
| Younger adult → adult listener; `부모님`, `할머니`, or `할아버지` is subject | kin title plus `-시-`, `계시다`, `드시다`, `주무시다`, or `말씀하시다` | Older family subject warrants ordinary family-politeness honorification. | 24–29, 31–32 |
| Adult speaker → adult listener; respected person's name/age is the topic | inherited `성함`, `연세`, respectful people `분`, and justified `-시-` | Reinforces high-value respectful personal information; `세` alone is not honorific. | 24–25, 27, 31–32 |
| Respectful subject in a noun modifier | the same allowed titles/kin terms plus justified modifier `-시-` or lexical honorific | Honorification remains attached to the respected subject within the modifier. | 21, 24–29, 31–32 |
| Adult speaker → teacher/manager/older relative; speaker is subject | fixed `말씀드려요` in ordinary polite context or `말씀드립니다` in a brief formal report | A fixed humble act of telling lowers the speaker; it is not generalized. | 25, 29, 31–32 |
| Service staff → customer; staff is subject | fixed `확인해 드립니다` in a customer-facing formal status update | A single high-value service formula; no productive `-아/어 드리다` conversion. | 25, 29, 31–32 |

Reject `-시-` on the speaker's own action, automatic honorification of every listener, double `-시-` on lexical honorifics, casual address to a superior, title padding, and accidental speech-level mixing.

| Public/service/school notice or announcement | 1–3 short sentences | formal declarative/question as applicable, limited instruction, schedule/number grouping | consistent `합니다체` | notice, announcement | 22–23, 29, 31–32 |
| Customer-facing or school/work status update | 1–3 short sentences | formal declarative, motivated past, respectful subject if justified, fixed humble chunk only where assigned | consistent `합니다체` | phone, reception, brief report | 22–25, 29, 31–32 |

## Binding pronunciation and orthography inventory

| Recurring context | Binding treatment | Slice assignments |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Plain, aspirated, and tense consonants | Recover a known or newly accepted word from the full situation; preserve lexical onset spelling. | 1–32; concentrated 1–6, 9–16, 31–32 |
| Major vowel spellings | Use lexical/semantic context for overlapping ordinary pronunciations; never make an audio-only spelling choice. | 1–32; concentrated 3, 7–10, 14–21, 26–30 |
| Simple and common complex 받침 | Retain the accepted underlying final through particles, endings, modifiers, formal endings, and quotation. | 1–32; concentrated 1–13, 17–28, 31–32 |
| Resyllabification | Hear a final before a vowel-initial particle/ending without moving it in writing. | 1–21, 24–32 |
| Nasalization | Recognize common final-plus-nasal contexts in requirements, plans, products, transport, and messages. | 1–16, 22–32 |
| Tensification | Recognize post-final, compound, number/counter, comparison, and formal-ending contexts without inserting an unlicensed doubled consonant. | 1–16, 22–23, 26–32 |
| Aspiration involving `ㅎ` | Preserve both underlying elements in accepted inflections and collocations. | 1–13, 17–25, 29–32 |
| `ㄴ/ㄹ` lateralization | Recover standard spelling in schedules, routes, quantities, formal settings, and reported messages. | 3–16, 20–23, 26–32 |
| Ordinary contractions | Hear natural standard polite contractions while typing their accepted orthographic form; reject pronunciation spellings. | 1–21, 24–32 |
| Formal-ending delivery | Produce clear `-습니다/ㅂ니다`, past formal, formal questions, and limited instructions with natural phrase-final contour. | 22–23, 25, 29, 31–32 |
| Noun-modifier boundary | Hear predicate ending plus following head noun as one noun phrase while preserving the written space before the head. | 17–21, 24, 26–32 |
| Number/counter and comparison grouping | Group number+counter/unit, comparison standard+`보다`, degree word, and limited phrase naturally; preserve spacing and attached particles. | 1–16, 22–23, 26–32 |
| Quotation boundary | Segment source/content/reporting predicate and distinguish quotation morphology without a stored voice or spelling change. | 26–29, 31–32 |
| Speech-act intonation | Distinguish statement, question, instruction, suggestion, and report using form, setting, punctuation, and contour together. | 1–32; explicit audit 23, 26–32 |
| Careful versus ordinary connected delivery | Accept both as listening delivery of the same standard answer; never add alternate phonetic text. | 1–32; explicit paired audit 31–32 |

## Lexical and accepted-target inventories

### Korean 00–01 audit baseline

Before this matrix was defined, every primary target in `cards/ko-KR/00.json` and `cards/ko-KR/01.json` was audited. The baseline contains exactly 1,300 cards: 300 Korean 00 and 1,000 Korean 01. Unicode NFC normalization, edge trimming, and internal-whitespace collapse produce 300 and 1,000 unique keys respectively, with zero within-stage duplicates and zero cross-stage intersections. All answers equal their clozes, occur exactly once in their sentence, contain no Latin letters, and have empty `accepted_answers`.

The full baseline stays external and must be loaded fresh for every generation and review batch. Copying its rows here would falsely make prerequisite material look like new Korean 02 coverage.

### Normalization and duplicate decisions

For each Korean 02 candidate:

1. Normalize `answer` to Unicode NFC, trim leading/trailing whitespace, and collapse every internal whitespace run to one ordinary space. Terminal punctuation is outside the answer by contract.
2. Reject an exact or normalized match against all 1,300 Korean 00–01 answers and all accepted earlier Korean 02 targets.
3. Build a semantic signature from canonical lemma or fixed chunk, sense, construction, communicative function, speech act, argument pattern, register/relationship, and, when relevant, modifier head or quotation source/recipient. Reject semantic duplication even when inflection, particle, word order, speech level, or a larger hidden span differs.
4. Audit each batch for mechanical series: form-only, tense-only, polarity-only, modifier-head-only, quotation-source-only, speech-level-only, name-only, noun-only, activity-only, country-only, number-only, date-only, price-only, route-only, size-only, and counter-only changes do not establish breadth without a distinct assigned function or contrast.
5. Do not rescue a duplicate by hiding an arbitrary larger phrase or sentence around it.
6. Add rows to both indexes only after the separate fresh Korean-editor review, source-contract audit, and exact/normalized/semantic/mechanical duplicate audit accept the corrected card. Drafts and rejected cards never enter either index.
7. Before generating slice `n`, supply the fresh session with all 1,300 baseline keys and exactly the accepted Korean 02 rows from slices lower than `n`. The current slice cannot treat another draft in its batch as a prerequisite.

### Accepted primary-target index format

This index is intentionally empty at matrix creation. It gains exactly one row per accepted card, in ID order, and therefore exactly 40 rows after each accepted slice. `normalized_primary_target` is the unique comparison key; `semantic_signature` records the independent coverage reason.

```text
card_id | normalized_primary_target | target_kind | canonical_lemma_or_fixed_chunk | construction_or_function | semantic_signature | slice | accepted_review
```

| card_id | normalized_primary_target | target_kind | canonical_lemma_or_fixed_chunk | construction_or_function | semantic_signature | slice | accepted_review |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | ---: | --- |

### Deduplicated lexical/chunk index format

This separate index is evidence-driven. It counts only useful lexical items and reusable chunks attested in accepted Korean 02 cards, after merging inflections and mechanical variants.

```text
canonical_entry | entry_kind | operational_sense_or_function | first_accepted_card_id | accepted_card_ids | attested_forms | slices | evidence_count
```

- `entry_kind` is `lemma`, `compound`, `collocation`, or `fixed_chunk`.
- Inflections of one lemma share a row unless accepted cards establish a genuinely distinct lexicalized operational sense.
- Particles, tense, polarity, speech level, honorification, quotation wrapping, modifier ending, counter value, and number value do not automatically create new lexical rows.
- A multiword chunk is indexed only when independently reusable and naturally attested; an arbitrary cloze span is not a chunk.
- `accepted_card_ids`, `attested_forms`, and `slices` are deduplicated cumulative sets. `evidence_count` equals the number of distinct accepted card IDs in that row.
- Every row points to at least one accepted Korean 02 card. Planned items, Korean 00–01-only items, and rejected drafts do not count.
- After each slice, merge its accepted evidence into existing rows before adding genuinely new rows, then report the cumulative deduplicated total. No target lexical-count quota may drive padding.

## Slice plan and prerequisite graph

### Rules shared by all slices

- Generate exactly the stated 40 sequential IDs. Each slice's disjoint primary-focus quotas sum to 40; secondary reinforcement never changes the quota assignment.
- Start a new no-context generation session with the complete issue, this matrix, the exact slice, the 1,300 Korean 00–01 accepted-target baseline, the accepted earlier Korean 02 target and lexical indexes, and the JSON contract. Use a separate new no-context Korean-editor session for that batch.
- Use only Korean 00–01 and accepted cards from the explicitly listed earlier Korean 02 slices as support. No same-slice draft, later slice, or merely planned item is prerequisite language.
- Keep one focused production target, useful visible context, and a complete fair audio sentence. `sentence` contains `cloze` exactly once; `answer` equals `cloze`; punctuation stays outside both.
- A cloze does not cross `.`, `?`, `!`, a quotation boundary, or a speaker-turn boundary. A whole utterance is hidden only when it is a compact fixed high-value expression.
- Keep `accepted_answers` empty unless the independent editor confirms a genuine standard orthographic alternative. Never add a pronunciation spelling. Do not add `reading` for Korean.
- Optional `grammar`, `register`, and `note` fields appear only when directly useful and are never empty strings. Relationship-sensitive fields identify speaker, listener, subject, and, for quotation, source/recipient.
- Audit naturalness, inflection, particles, spacing, speech level, honorification, modifier scope, quotation source, punctuation, cloze fairness, and exact/normalized/semantic/mechanical duplication before acceptance.
- Replace the draft batch with the fully corrected JSON and update both cumulative indexes only after independent acceptance. Run source validation, then generate and listen to several complete-sentence local audio samples with the fixed Korean configuration before starting the next slice; audio remains uncommitted.
- Answers always use standard spelling. Each slice audits its assigned sound behavior in careful and ordinary connected delivery and rejects Korean 03 leakage even when a later form would sound natural.
- Store no prompts, review transcripts, reports, review state, workflow infrastructure, or generated artifacts.

### Slice 1 — practical ability and access (`ko-02-0001`–`ko-02-0040`, 40 cards)

- Binding primary-focus allocation: 16 ability-form targets + 10 practical ability/access functions + 8 listening/orthography targets + 6 ability-question and actor checks = 40.
- Grammar and communicative targets: introduce `-(으)ㄹ 수 있어요/없어요` for learned ability, device/service operation, access, reservation, schedule availability, and feasible task completion; ask and answer high-frequency ability questions.
- Lexical domains and collocations: skills, language/task use, devices, files, bookings, building access, transport, service hours, time windows, and quantities; usable collocations include operating, opening, sending, reserving, entering, attending, and finishing within an inherited duration.
- Modifier/quotation/register/honorification: no new modifier or quotation system; ordinary `해요체` only. Inherited `-시-` may support a respected subject but is not a focus.
- Pronunciation, spelling, spacing, and numbers: preserve the space in `-(으)ㄹ 수 있어요/없어요`, stem/final selection, resyllabification, 받침, contractions, and meaningful duration/quantity grouping.
- Speaker/listener/subject relationships: adult learner or customer to adult peer, teacher, coworker, or staff; the person with or without ability is explicit or recoverable, and questions about the listener remain courteous.
- Listening target: identify the actor, task, and positive/negative possibility from a complete sentence and hear the `수` construction boundary in connected delivery.
- Production target: state or ask one practical ability/access fact with the correct form, spacing, and useful argument pattern.
- Prerequisite Korean 02 slices: none.
- Prerequisite Korean 00–01 material: Korean 00 slices 1–10 and Korean 01 slices 1–25 in full; especially Hangul/sound control, ordinary present, particles, questions, time/quantity systems, service domains, and inherited ability-adjacent `못` only as known contrast material.
- Leakage exclusions: no `못` versus ability contrast targets until slice 2, no `-(으)ㄹ 줄 알다/모르다`, permission system, formal style, advanced condition, or verb-substitution ability list.
- Accepted evidence/review marker: `slice-1-independent-ko-editor-pass-2026-08-05`. Final corrected source contains exactly 40 cards and accepted-primary rows with the binding disjoint allocation `16 + 10 + 8 + 6`; source-contract, role/register, Korean 02 leakage, and cumulative exact/normalized/semantic/mechanical duplicate audits passed with zero accepted-target collisions.

### Slice 2 — inability, failure, and possibility framing (`ko-02-0041`–`ko-02-0080`, 40 cards)

- Binding primary-focus allocation: 16 `못`/`-(으)ㄹ 수 없어요` contrast targets + 10 failure/circumstance functions + 8 listening/orthography targets + 6 polarity and framing checks = 40.
- Grammar and communicative targets: distinguish inherited `못` for one failed/blocked performance or ordinary inability from `-(으)ㄹ 수 없어요` for lack of capacity/possibility; retain `안` as a separate volitional/factual negative.
- Lexical domains and collocations: missed contact, failed submission, unavailable access, device or transport problems, health limits, schedule conflicts, service restrictions, and practical skills; include correction and concise reason context without excuse narratives.
- Modifier/quotation/register/honorification: none new; ordinary `해요체`. A respected third-person subject may use inherited `-시-` only when the relationship is clear.
- Pronunciation, spelling, spacing, and numbers: space `못` before the predicate, preserve the ability-construction spaces, hear failed past versus current inability, and group telephone/time/duration support without changing number values mechanically.
- Speaker/listener/subject relationships: adult peer, classmate, coworker, customer/staff, or traveler/staff; context identifies who attempted what and whether circumstances or capacity block it.
- Listening target: choose among volitional `안`, failed/circumstantial `못`, and possibility `-(으)ㄹ 수 없어요` from the event and time reference.
- Production target: frame one inability or failure naturally and, in contrast cards, change the framing rather than merely swap endings.
- Prerequisite Korean 02 slices: slice 1.
- Prerequisite Korean 00–01 material: all Korean 00–01; especially Korean 01 slice 19 `안/못`, past, repair, transport, health, work/school, and service failures.
- Leakage exclusions: no obligation, condition, advanced excuse/causal chain, ability paradigm, semantic duplicate pair, or `못` target already protected in Korean 01.
- Accepted evidence/review marker: `slice-2-independent-ko-editor-pass-2026-08-05`. Final corrected source contains exactly 40 cards and accepted-primary rows with the binding disjoint allocation `16 + 10 + 8 + 6`; source-contract, role/register, Korean 02 prerequisite and later-slice leakage, Hangul/spacing, and cumulative exact/normalized/semantic/mechanical duplicate audits passed with zero accepted-target collisions.

### Slice 3 — personal necessity and practical obligation (`ko-02-0081`–`ko-02-0120`, 40 cards)

- Binding primary-focus allocation: 16 `-아/어야 해요` targets + 10 duty/deadline functions + 8 listening/orthography targets + 6 obligation-source checks = 40.
- Grammar and communicative targets: introduce `-아/어야 해요` for personal necessity, situational requirement, deadline, preparation, appointment, health routine, travel requirement, household duty, and school/work procedure.
- Lexical domains and collocations: preparing documents/materials, submitting by a deadline, arriving on time, carrying a required item, taking medicine, resting, checking a booking, paying, cleaning, and contacting a responsible person.
- Modifier/quotation/register/honorification: no new system; default ordinary `해요체`. Inherited honorification is support only; obligations about another adult must be impersonal, questioned from the speaker's perspective, or appropriately softened.
- Pronunciation, spelling, spacing, and numbers: preserve conjugation and the space before `해요`, hear aspiration/tensification and ordinary contractions, and group deadline dates, times, quantities, doses, and durations naturally.
- Speaker/listener/subject relationships: speaker describes own duty, asks staff what the speaker must do, or states an impersonal shared procedure to an adult peer; no decontextualized command to a superior.
- Listening target: identify who has the duty, the required action, and whether its source is personal, situational, or external.
- Production target: state or ask about one credible requirement with correct conjugation, spacing, and pragmatic force.
- Prerequisite Korean 02 slices: slices 1–2.
- Prerequisite Korean 00–01 material: all Korean 00–01; especially requests/instructions, dates/times/counters, health, travel, school/work, household tasks, and ordinary reasons.
- Leakage exclusions: no `-아/어야 돼요` target until slice 4, condition, formal regulation, legal vocabulary, blunt adult-directed mandate, or duty-verb substitution series.
- Accepted evidence/review marker: `slice-3-independent-ko-editor-02-pass-2026-08-05`. A fresh no-generation-context Korean-editor pass reviewed all 40 cards against Korean 00–01 and accepted Slices 1–2. Corrections were made to `ko-02-0082`, `ko-02-0086`, `ko-02-0091`, `ko-02-0093`, `ko-02-0094`, `ko-02-0096`–`ko-02-0098`, `ko-02-0103`–`ko-02-0104`, `ko-02-0106`, `ko-02-0110`, `ko-02-0112`–`ko-02-0115`, `ko-02-0119`, and `ko-02-0120`; sentence text changed in all of those except `ko-02-0115`. The final source and accepted-primary rows retain the binding allocation `16 + 10 + 8 + 6`; contract, role/register, prerequisite/leakage, spelling/spacing/sound, and cumulative exact/normalized/semantic/mechanical audits passed with an accepted duplicate count of zero.

### Slice 4 — spoken obligation, requirements, and ordinary rules (`ko-02-0121`–`ko-02-0160`, 40 cards)

- Binding primary-focus allocation: 16 `-아/어야 돼요` and obligation-contrast targets + 10 rule/procedure functions + 8 listening/orthography targets + 6 politeness/source checks = 40.
- Grammar and communicative targets: introduce common spoken `-아/어야 돼요`, consolidate `-아/어야 해요`, and distinguish personal need, situational requirement, external procedure, and ordinary rule without assigning false categorical meanings to the two endings.
- Lexical domains and collocations: reception procedures, school/work entry or submission, transport and reservation requirements, household rules, food/health routines, deadlines, identification, payment, and equipment preparation.
- Modifier/quotation/register/honorification: none new; ordinary `해요체`. Inherited fixed `안 돼요` may support a separate prohibition but `-(으)면 안 돼요` waits for slice 6.
- Pronunciation, spelling, spacing, and numbers: preserve the standard contracted spelling in `돼요`, the space before `돼요/해요`, irregular stems, and date/time/counter phrase grouping.
- Speaker/listener/subject relationships: adult asks what is required, peer reminds a peer gently, or staff explains an impersonal procedure; subject and responsible actor are recoverable.
- Listening target: hear both accepted obligation endings in ordinary delivery and use context to recover duty source rather than invent a rigid register rule.
- Production target: select a natural obligation form for one practical procedure and phrase any adult-directed reminder appropriately.
- Prerequisite Korean 02 slices: slices 1–3.
- Prerequisite Korean 00–01 material: all Korean 00–01; especially `돼요` spelling, service instructions, requests, rules as fixed support, time/quantity systems, and subject omission.
- Leakage exclusions: no conditional prohibition, formal legal rule, bureaucratic notice, advanced softening, obligation-only minimal pairs, or speech-level conversion.
- Accepted evidence/review marker: `slice-4-independent-ko-editor-pass-2026-08-05`. ### Slice 5 — real conditions for choices and procedures (`ko-02-0161`–`ko-02-0200`, 40 cards)

- Speaker/listener/subject relationships: adult peers deciding together, customer asking staff, staff explaining a service condition, or household members discussing a likely result; the conditional actor is explicit or recoverable.
- Listening target: distinguish a possible trigger from an already-realized reason and identify which clause is condition and which is result.
- Production target: give one credible real condition that changes a practical choice, action, availability, or procedure.

- Prerequisite Korean 00–01 material: all Korean 00–01; especially future plans, requests/instructions, `-아서/어서`, weather, schedules, shopping, transport, health, and service availability.

### Slice 14 — practical comparison with `보다`, `더`, and `덜` (`ko-02-0521`–`ko-02-0560`, 40 cards)

- Pronunciation, spelling, spacing, and numbers: attach `보다` to the standard, group `더/덜` with the predicate, preserve number/counter/unit spacing and tensification, and recover standard spelling from connected comparison phrases.
- Speaker/listener/subject relationships: customer/staff, traveler/staff, adult peers choosing, or coworkers comparing schedules; comparison serves the current participant's practical choice.
- Listening target: identify the comparison standard, scale, direction, and selected option despite flexible Korean word order.
- Production target: make one natural two-option comparison that supports a credible choice, using meaningful numeric detail only when needed.
- Prerequisite Korean 02 slices: slices 1–13.

### Binding audit gates

- **Range and quota arithmetic:** all 32 slice headings must match the formula; IDs have no gap/overlap; every slice has disjoint quotas totaling 40; each accepted slice adds exactly 40 accepted-primary rows.
- **Baseline audit:** load all 1,300 Korean 00–01 normalized answers for every batch. Confirm Korean 02 has zero exact/normalized intersections and independently audit semantic or disguised mechanical reuse.
- **Construction evidence:** every construction-assignment row needs natural accepted evidence in an assigned introduction slice and its cumulative audit slices. A label or ending-only pair is insufficient.
- **Contrast evidence:** every binding contrast row needs situations that make both readings materially distinct; do not count a false minimal pair or mechanical speech-level/tense swap.
- **Noun modifiers:** accepted evidence covers each predicate class, time/aspect, head-noun family, irregular group, and error boundary. Each card has a useful complete situation and unambiguous head/scope.
- **Quotation:** every form has accepted source/recipient relationships, speech-act evidence, correct punctuation, and no nested or ambiguous report. Direct and reported speech acts remain distinguishable.
- **Register and honorification:** all productive formal forms, allowed titles/addresses, `-시-`, lexical honorifics, and three fixed humble chunks receive assigned evidence. Speaker, listener, subject, and setting are recoverable; formal utterances never mix levels.
- **Narration and messages:** every task/medium row receives evidence within its maximum discourse length and connector inventory. No cloze crosses a boundary and no card hides a passage.
- **Numbers and pronunciation:** every row in both binding inventories receives natural accepted evidence. Values and sound contexts solve real tasks rather than pad the count; answers always use standard spelling.
- **Lexical index:** every counted lemma/chunk is attested, deduplicated after inflection/mechanical variants, and linked to accepted Korean 02 card IDs. The cumulative count is reported after each slice but never used as a padding target.
- **Prerequisite order:** slice 1 uses Korean 00–01 only; slice `n` uses only Korean 00–01 and accepted Korean 02 slices lower than `n`. Search every card for later-slice construction leakage before acceptance.
- **Card contract:** `sentence` contains `cloze` exactly once; `answer` equals `cloze`; all required strings are nonempty; optional fields are useful/nonempty; `accepted_answers` is normally empty; punctuation and quotation/turn boundaries remain outside the target.
- **Originality:** normalized primary targets are globally unique across Korean 00–02. Semantic and mechanical audits reject arbitrary larger clozes, form-only conversions, and substitutions of names, nouns, numbers, dates, prices, routes, sizes, counters, modifier heads, quotation sources, or speech levels.
- **Exclusions:** stage-wide and per-slice exclusions are binding. A natural sentence is still rejected if it imports Korean 03 grammar, discourse, stance, voice, register, or infrastructure.

After slice 32, run twelve separate fresh cumulative Korean-editor reviews and correct source cards directly for: (1) ability and `못`; (2) obligation and conditions; (3) intention, attempt, and experience; (4) before/after and sequence; (5) comparison and limits; (6) noun-modifying clauses; (7) productive formal polite speech; (8) introductory quotation; (9) honorification and relationships; (10) connected narration and messages; (11) pronunciation, spelling, spacing, numbers, and punctuation; and (12) cloze fairness, normalized uniqueness, semantic/mechanical duplication, and Korean 03 leakage. ### Why exactly 1,280 cards completes coverage without padding

The count follows the work. The first 640 cards give four full slices each to ability/obligation and conditions/intentions, five to attempts/experience/temporal sequence, and three to comparison/limits. The next 360 give five slices to the four productive noun-modifier systems and their high-risk integration, plus four slices to productive formal polite speech and expanded but controlled honorification. The next 120 isolate all introductory quotation speech acts. Eighty cards then practice practical messages and bounded connected narration, and the final eighty close concrete number, pronunciation, register, relationship, contrast, and duplicate-audit gaps.

Each 40-card slice has disjoint substantive quotas and a distinct operational burden. No quota is filled by conjugation tables, translating Korean 01 into another speech level, changing only a head noun/source/name/number, or hiding a larger duplicate. Conversely, reaching `ko-02-1280` does not complete the stage if any construction, contrast, modifier, quotation form, formal/honorific relationship, narration/message task, number function, pronunciation context, lexical/index rule, prerequisite check, or exclusion lacks accepted evidence. This supports the preferred 32 × 40 plan without inventing coverage to approach the upper planning limit.
